Probabilistic <sub>g</sub>ra<sub>p</sub>hical models (PGMs) com<sub>p</sub>actl<sub>y</sub> encode structured joint distributions, but Most Probable Ex<sub>p</sub>lanation (MPE), which finds the hi<sub>g</sub>hest-<sub>p</sub>robabilit<sub>y</sub> assi<sub>g</sub>nment to unobserved variables, <sub>rema</sub>i<sub>ns com u</sub>t<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ona</sub>ll i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>rac</sub>t<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> i<sub>n</sub> l<sub>ar e,</sub> d<sub>ense</sub>l <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>t<sub>ure</sub>d <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s.</sub> A<sub>n</sub> ti<sub>me s s</sub>t<sub>ema</sub>ti<sub>c searc</sub>h<sub>,</sub> such as branch-and-bound (B&B), is a <sub>p</sub>o<sub>p</sub>ular a<sub>pp</sub>roach, but its eficienc<sub>y</sub> de<sub>p</sub>ends on ti<sub>g</sub>ht admissible <sub>upper</sub> b<sub>oun</sub>d<sub>s</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>are o</sub>ft<sub>en pro</sub>hibiti<sub>ve</sub>l<sub>y expens</sub>i<sub>ve</sub> t<sub>o compu</sub>t<sub>e us</sub>i<sub>ng c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>me</sub>th<sub>o</sub>d<sub>s</sub> lik<sub>e</sub> Mi<sub>n</sub>i<sub>-</sub>B<sub>uc</sub>k<sub>e</sub>t Elimination (MBE). A <sub>p</sub>robabilistic <sub>g</sub>ra<sub>p</sub>hical model (PGM), <sub>g</sub>iven b<sub>y</sub> the tu<sub>p</sub>le $( G , F , X , D )$ , represents the joint distribution of a collection of random variables that factorizes over a graph G where $F = \{ f _ { 1 } , . . . , f _ { m } \}$ i<sub>s a se</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f f<sub>ac</sub>t<sub>ors,</sub> i<sub>.e., non-nega</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> f<sub>unc</sub>ti<sub>ons over</sub> th<sub>e var</sub>i<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>es</sub> $X = \{ X _ { 1 } , . . . , X _ { n } \}$ <sub>w</sub>ith di<sub>scre</sub>t<sub>e</sub> d<sub>oma</sub>i<sub>ns</sub> $D = \{ D _ { 1 } , \ldots , D _ { n } \}$ that define a factorization of the joint distribution $\begin{array} { r } { p ( x _ { 1 } , \ldots , x _ { n } ) \propto \prod _ { i = 1 } ^ { m } f _ { i } ( x ) } \end{array}$ . (Koller and Friedman, 2009; Darwiche, 2009)

## Monotonicity in Evidence

Th<sub>e op</sub>ti<sub>ma</sub>l l<sub>og-</sub>lik<sub>e</sub>lih<sub>oo</sub>d <sub>score</sub> $h ^ { \ast } ( x _ { E } )$ in the MPE settings is monotone non-increasing in evidence, i.e., f<sub>or</sub> $E \subseteq E ^ { \prime }$ <sub>w</sub>ith $x _ { E ^ { \prime } } | _ { E } = x _ { E } , h ^ { * } ( x _ { E ^ { \prime } } ) \leq h ^ { * } ( x _ { E } )$ <sub>.</sub> Thi<sub>s</sub> i<sub>s</sub> <sub>a</sub> d<sub>es</sub>i<sub>ra</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> <sub>proper</sub>t<sub>y</sub> <sub>o</sub>f <sub>a</sub> <sub>pre</sub>di<sub>c</sub>t<sub>or</sub> <sub>as</sub> <sub>see</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>more ev</sub>id<sub>ence can on</sub>l<sub>y re</sub>d<sub>uce</sub> th<sub>e upper</sub> b<sub>oun</sub>d<sub>.</sub> A<sub>s suc</sub>h<sub>, we requ</sub>i<sub>re</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>our pre</sub>di<sub>c</sub>t<sub>ors are a</sub>l<sub>so mono</sub>t<sub>one</sub> n<sub>o</sub>n<sub>-</sub>in<sub>c</sub>r<sub>eas</sub>in<sub>g</sub> in <sub>ev</sub>id<sub>e</sub>n<sub>ce,</sub> mirr<sub>o</sub>rin<sub>g</sub> th<sub>e</sub> b<sub>e</sub>h<sub>av</sub>i<sub>o</sub>r <sub>o</sub>f MBE/JGLP <sub>a</sub>nd <sub>p</sub>r<sub>ese</sub>r<sub>v</sub>in<sub>g co</sub>rr<sub>ec</sub>tn<sub>ess a</sub>l<sub>o</sub>n<sub>g</sub> B&B <sub>searc</sub>h <sub>pa</sub>th<sub>s.</sub>

While standard Monotonic Neural Networks (MNNs) can <sub>g</sub>uarantee monotonicit<sub>y</sub> with res<sub>p</sub>ect to in<sub>p</sub>ut <sub>va</sub>l<sub>ues</sub> $( \mathbf { z } \leq \mathbf { z } ^ { \prime } \implies f ( \mathbf { z } ) \leq f ( \mathbf { z } ^ { \prime } ) )$ , achieving monotonicity with respect to the presence of evidence <sub>requ</sub>i<sub>res a mo</sub>difi<sub>e</sub>d <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>t<sub>egy: we</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>ro</sub>d<sub>uce an</sub> i<sub>npu</sub>t <sub>enco</sub>di<sub>ng</sub> t<sub>ec</sub>h<sub>n</sub>i<sub>que</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>re</sub>d<sub>uces ev</sub>id<sub>ence-mono</sub>t<sub>on</sub>i<sub>c</sub>it<sub>y</sub> t<sub>o</sub> <sub>va</sub>l<sub>ue-mono</sub>t<sub>on</sub>i<sub>c</sub>it<sub>y.</sub> O<sub>ur approac</sub>h <sub>re</sub>li<sub>es on</sub> th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>o</sub>ll<sub>ow</sub>i<sub>ng s</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>t<sub>ura</sub>l <sub>pr</sub>i<sub>nc</sub>i<sub>p</sub>l<sub>e.</sub>

Lemma 1. Let $\phi ( X )$ be an encoding of a discrete variable X taking values in the set D, and let e represent the encoding ofthe “unassigned” state. Ifϕ satisfies,

(i) Strict Dominance of Evidence: $\phi ( j ) > \mathbf { e }$ (element-wise) for all $j \in D$

(ii) Value Incomparability: $\phi ( i ) \not \le \phi ( j )$ (element-wise) for all distinct $i , j \in D .$

Then, a value-monotonic neural network operating on the concatenated input $\mathbf { z } = [ \phi ( X _ { 1 } ) , \ldots , \phi ( X _ { n } ) ]$ is guaranteed to be monotone non-decreasing with respect to the inclusion of evidence and the encoding does not create a spurious monotonic relationship between diferent values ofX.

A natural family of encodings that satisfy these properties is the k-hot scheme, where each value of $X _ { i }$ is mapped to a unique vector of length M containing exactly k ones, and the unassigned sentinel e is the all-zeros vector. Pro<sub>p</sub>ert<sub>y</sub> (i) is satisfied as an<sub>y</sub> vector with $k \geq 1$ <sub>ones</sub> <sub>s</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>c</sub>tl<sub>y</sub> d<sub>om</sub>i<sub>na</sub>t<sub>es</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>zero</sub> <sub>vec</sub>t<sub>or,</sub> <sub>an</sub>d propert<sub>y</sub> (ii) follows from the fact that no k-hot vector can be a subset of another distinct k-hot vector. This is th<sub>e enco</sub>di<sub>ng we a</sub>d<sub>op</sub>t h<sub>ere.</sub>

We instantiate L2B usin<sub>g</sub> a Scalable Monotonic Neural Network (SMNN) architecture (Kim and Lee, 2024). Specifically, to achieve the final goal of a monotone non-increasing bound, we negate the final output,

$$
f _ { \theta } ( \mathbf { z } ) = - W _ { L } ^ { + } \sigma \Bigl ( \mathrm { B N } \bigl ( W _ { L - 1 } ^ { + } \sigma ( \dots W _ { 1 } ^ { + } \mathbf { z } ) \bigr ) \Bigr ) .\tag{1}
$$

The exp <sub>reparame</sub>t<sub>er</sub>i<sub>za</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>p</sub>l<sub>aces</sub> <sub>ou</sub>t<sub>pu</sub>t<sub>-</sub>l<sub>ayer</sub> <sub>we</sub>i<sub>g</sub>ht<sub>s</sub> <sub>a</sub>t $e ^ { W ^ { \prime } }$ <sub>,</sub> <sub>w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h <sub>causes</sub> <sub>ou</sub>t<sub>pu</sub>t <sub>magn</sub>it<sub>u</sub>d<sub>es</sub> t<sub>o</sub> <sub>co</sub>ll<sub>apse</sub> d<sub>ur</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> trainin<sub>g</sub>. Th<sub>e ca</sub>lib<sub>ra</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d b<sub>oun</sub>d $g$ <sub>sa</sub>ti<sub>s</sub>fi<sub>es</sub> th<sub>e per-no</sub>d<sub>e coverage</sub> $\mathbb { P } [ g \ge h ^ { * } ] \ge \gamma$ <sub>,</sub> b<sub>u</sub>t <sub>a s</sub>i<sub>ng</sub>l<sub>e no</sub>d<sub>e w</sub>h<sub>ose</sub> b<sub>oun</sub>d <sub>un</sub>d<sub>eres</sub>ti<sub>ma</sub>t<sub>es</sub> $h ^ { * }$ <sub>can prune</sub> th<sub>e op</sub>ti<sub>ma</sub>l <sub>su</sub>bt<sub>ree.</sub> With<sub>ou</sub>t <sub>prun</sub>i<sub>ng, exac</sub>t<sub>ness o</sub>f AOBB i<sub>s preserve</sub>d b<sub>y</sub> construction and the only efect is on anytime solution quality. When using pruning, γ trades of between violation probability and speed (higher γ produces looser bounds but stronger guarantees while lower $\gamma$ <sub>p</sub>roduces ti<sub>g</sub>hter bounds that ma<sub>y p</sub>rune o<sub>p</sub>timal re<sub>g</sub>ions).

We evaluated L2B on 30 <sub>p</sub>robabilistic <sub>g</sub>ra<sub>p</sub>hical models from the UAI inference com<sub>p</sub>etitions (Go<sub>g</sub>ate, 2014; Dechter et al., 2022). The benchmark suite contains five model families with induced widths between 10 <sub>an</sub>d 100<sub>:</sub> B<sub>ayes</sub>i<sub>an ne</sub>t<sub>wor</sub>k<sub>s, gr</sub>id<sub>s, segmen</sub>t<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s, ran</sub>d<sub>om un</sub>if<sub>orm mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s, an</sub>d <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>t<sub>ure</sub>d <sub>pre</sub>di<sub>c</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s.</sub> T<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> 6 i<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e appen</sub>di<sub>x repor</sub>t<sub>s</sub> d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>a</sub>il<sub>e</sub>d i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>orma</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> f<sub>or a</sub>ll i<sub>ns</sub>t<sub>ances.</sub>

F<sub>or eac</sub>h b<sub>enc</sub>h<sub>mar</sub>k PGM<sub>, we genera</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d 20<sub>,</sub>000 t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>ng examp</sub>l<sub>es us</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> Al<sub>gor</sub>ith<sub>m</sub> 1<sub>.</sub> W<sub>e o</sub>bt<sub>a</sub>i<sub>ne</sub>d <sub>par</sub>ti<sub>a</sub>l assi<sub>g</sub>nments via Gibbs sam<sub>p</sub>lin<sub>g</sub> with 1,000 burn-in ste<sub>p</sub>s and an ex<sub>p</sub>onential size distribution (bias 2.0), s<sub>p</sub>annin<sub>g</sub> evidence ratios 0.1–0.9. We com<sub>p</sub>ute <sub>g</sub>round-truth labels usin<sub>g</sub> DAOOPT (Marinescu and Dechter, 2005) with i-bound 10, requiring 0.5–300 seconds per quer<sub>y</sub>. For computationall<sub>y</sub> intensive multi-domain instances (Object Detection, Protein Folding), we used evidence ratio 0.5–0.9 and i-bound 6 to ensure tractable label <sub>g</sub>eneration; all baseline com<sub>p</sub>arisons use i-bound 4. We s<sub>p</sub>lit the data into trainin<sub>g</sub> (50%), validation (10%), calibration (20%), and test (20%). We held out the calibration set from trainin<sub>g</sub> and used it onl<sub>y</sub> to <sub>es</sub>ti<sub>ma</sub>t<sub>e ca</sub>lib<sub>ra</sub>ti<sub>on parame</sub>t<sub>ers.</sub>

The se aration is cate orical rather than statistical. SMNN records 0/3000 violations, both raw and after <sub>ca</sub>lib<sub>ra</sub>ti<sub>on,</sub> i<sub>n every</sub> f<sub>am</sub>il<sub>y.</sub> UMLP<sub>,</sub> th<sub>e same</sub> d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>a,</sub> th<sub>e same ca</sub>lib<sub>ra</sub>ti<sub>on, an uncons</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>ne</sub>d <sub>arc</sub>hit<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>ure v</sub>i<sub>o</sub>l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>es</sub> in 849/3000 <sub>p</sub>airs raw (28.3%) and 823/3000 after calibration (27.4%), with <sub>p</sub>er-famil<sub>y</sub> rates from 15.7% on random-uniform instances to 59.0% on Object Detection. We consider the trade favorable for search: a non-monotone bound can prune the optimum deterministically through a single inversion at a bin boundary, whereas an under-covered monotone bound does so onl robabilisticall , at a rate γ controls.

## 6 Conclusion

We introduced Learnin<sub>g</sub> to Bound (L2B), a framework for <sub>p</sub>redictin<sub>g</sub> ti<sub>g</sub>ht u<sub>pp</sub>er bounds on MPE values with f<sub>orma</sub>l <sub>s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>guaran</sub>t<sub>ees.</sub> B<sub>y</sub> t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>ng on</sub> hi<sub>g</sub>h<sub>-qua</sub>lit<sub>y superv</sub>i<sub>s</sub>i<sub>on</sub> f<sub>rom expens</sub>i<sub>ve</sub> MBE <sub>con</sub>fi<sub>gura</sub>ti<sub>ons</sub> (i-bound 10) and applying conformal calibration, L2B produces bounds that are simultaneously tight (5–10× lower gap than MBE), fast (milliseconds vs. minutes, enabling deployment in search inner loops), and valid with hi<sub>g</sub>h <sub>p</sub>robabilit<sub>y</sub> (finite-sam<sub>p</sub>le covera<sub>g</sub>e <sub>g</sub>uarantees, with a measured and γ-controllable violation rate). Th<sub>e</sub> k<sub>ey emp</sub>i<sub>r</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l fi<sub>n</sub>di<sub>ng</sub> i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>neura</sub>l b<sub>oun</sub>d<sub>s prov</sub>id<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e grea</sub>t<sub>es</sub>t b<sub>ene</sub>fit <sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> th<sub>ey are mos</sub>t <sub>nee</sub>d<sub>e</sub>d<sub>: on</sub> high-treewidth instances where classical bounds are loosest. When integrated into AOBB, neural bounds <sub>ac</sub>hi<sub>eve</sub> 1<sub>–</sub>6% b<sub>e</sub>tt<sub>er</sub> MPE <sub>so</sub>l<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>ons</sub> <sub>on</sub> hi<sub>g</sub>h<sub>-</sub>t<sub>reew</sub>idth R<sub>an</sub>d<sub>om</sub> U<sub>n</sub>if<sub>orm</sub> <sub>an</sub>d l<sub>arge</sub> <sub>gr</sub>id i<sub>ns</sub>t<sub>ances</sub> $( w ^ { * } \geq 6 0 )$ <sub>w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h d<sub>emons</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>t<sub>es</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t ti<sub>g</sub>ht b<sub>oun</sub>d<sub>s ena</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> di<sub>scovery o</sub>f hi<sub>g</sub>h<sub>-qua</sub>lit<sub>y reg</sub>i<sub>ons more qu</sub>i<sub>c</sub>kl<sub>y</sub> th<sub>an</sub> l<sub>ooser</sub> <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l b<sub>oun</sub>d<sub>s,</sub> <sub>w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h <sub>was</sub>t<sub>e</sub> ti<sub>me</sub> <sub>exp</sub>l<sub>or</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>su</sub>b<sub>op</sub>ti<sub>ma</sub>l <sub>searc</sub>h <sub>pa</sub>th<sub>s.</sub>

Limitations L2B specializes a predictor to each PGM with fixed structure and parameters, favoring tight i<sub>ns</sub>t<sub>ance-spec</sub>ifi<sub>c</sub> b<sub>oun</sub>d<sub>s over cross-mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l <sub>genera</sub>li<sub>za</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub> C<sub>on</sub>f<sub>orma</sub>l <sub>ca</sub>lib<sub>ra</sub>ti<sub>on prov</sub>id<sub>es</sub> fi<sub>n</sub>it<sub>e-samp</sub>l<sub>e</sub> coverage at each search node; although this guarantee does not extend jointly over entire search paths, AOBB remains anytime and preserves the feasibility and objective value of incumbent solutions.

T<sub>o</sub> f<sub>ur</sub>th<sub>er c</sub>l<sub>ar</sub>if<sub>y</sub> th<sub>e en</sub>d<sub>-</sub>t<sub>o-en</sub>d <sub>execu</sub>ti<sub>on o</sub>f <sub>our</sub> f<sub>ramewor</sub>k<sub>, we prov</sub>id<sub>e a</sub> d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>a</sub>il<sub>e</sub>d <sub>v</sub>i<sub>sua</sub>li<sub>za</sub>ti<sub>on o</sub>f th<sub>e</sub> Learnin<sub>g</sub> to Bound (L2B) <sub>p</sub>i<sub>p</sub>eline in Fi<sub>g</sub>ure 2. This dia<sub>g</sub>ram illustrates the three distinct <sub>p</sub>hases of our method: <sub>enco</sub>di<sub>ng, neura</sub>l <sub>pre</sub>di<sub>c</sub>ti<sub>on, an</sub>d <sub>s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>ca</sub>lib<sub>ra</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub>

A<sub>s</sub> d<sub>ep</sub>i<sub>c</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d i<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>op</sub> <sub>pane</sub>l<sub>,</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>process</sub> b<sub>eg</sub>i<sub>ns</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>rans</sub>f<sub>orma</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>o</sub>f <sub>a</sub> <sub>par</sub>ti<sub>a</sub>l <sub>ass</sub>i<sub>gnmen</sub>t $x _ { E }$ i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>o</sub> <sub>a</sub> hi<sub>g</sub>h<sub>-</sub>di<sub>mens</sub>i<sub>ona</sub>l <sub>vec</sub>t<sub>or</sub> $\mathbf { z } _ { x _ { E } }$ <sub>v</sub>i<sub>a</sub> th<sub>e enco</sub>di<sub>ng sc</sub>h<sub>eme</sub> d<sub>e</sub>fi<sub>ne</sub>d i<sub>n</sub> L<sub>emma</sub> 1<sub>.</sub> Thi<sub>s vec</sub>t<sub>or</sub> i<sub>s processe</sub>d b<sub>y</sub> th<sub>e</sub> SMNN<sub>, w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h <sub>amor</sub>ti<sub>zes</sub> th<sub>e</sub> hi<sub>g</sub>h <sub>cos</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l MPE b<sub>oun</sub>d<sub>s</sub> b<sub>y pre</sub>di<sub>c</sub>ti<sub>ng a raw</sub> l<sub>og-</sub>lik<sub>e</sub>lih<sub>oo</sub>d <sub>score</sub> $\hat { h } ( x _ { E } )$ i<sub>n m</sub>illi<sub>secon</sub>d<sub>s.</sub>

The middle and bottom panels visualize our primary mechanism for ensuring admissibility: stratified conformal calibration. Because raw neural predictions lack formal guarantees, we compute nonconformity scores $s _ { i }$ on a held-out calibration set. By stratifying these scores into K bins based on the evidence size $| x _ { E } |$ <sub>,</sub> <sub>we</sub> <sub>ex</sub>t<sub>rac</sub>t <sub>per-</sub>bi<sub>n</sub> <sub>quan</sub>til<sub>es</sub> $\hat { q } _ { \gamma } ^ { b }$ th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>cap</sub>t<sub>ure</sub> h<sub>ow</sub> <sub>pre</sub>di<sub>c</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>uncer</sub>t<sub>a</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>y</sub> <sub>evo</sub>l<sub>ves</sub> d<sub>ur</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>searc</sub>h<sub>.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> fi<sub>na</sub>l <sub>ca</sub>lib<sub>ra</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d b<sub>oun</sub>d $g ( x _ { E } )$ i<sub>s</sub> f<sub>orme</sub>d b<sub>y</sub> <sub>a</sub>ddi<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>appropr</sub>i<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub> bi<sub>n-spec</sub>ifi<sub>c</sub> <sub>quan</sub>til<sub>e</sub> t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>neura</sub>l <sub>ou</sub>t<sub>pu</sub>t<sub>.</sub> This provides a formal coverage guarantee—ensuring the bound is valid with probability at least γ—while <sub>a</sub>d<sub>ap</sub>ti<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e marg</sub>i<sub>n</sub> t<sub>o</sub> b<sub>e</sub> ti<sub>g</sub>ht<sub>er</sub> i<sub>n reg</sub>i<sub>ons w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> th<sub>e mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l i<sub>s more con</sub>fid<sub>en</sub>t<sub>.</sub>

## D Benchmark Details

We evaluated on 30 <sub>p</sub>robabilistic <sub>g</sub>ra<sub>p</sub>hical models from the UAI inference com<sub>p</sub>etitions (Go<sub>g</sub>ate, 2014; Dechter et al., 2022). The benchmark suite contains five model families with induced widths between 10 and 100: (1) Bayesian Networks, comprising six real-world instances (BN\_9 through BN\_70) with 90–2315 <sub>var</sub>i<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>es an</sub>d i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>uce</sub>d <sub>w</sub>idth<sub>s</sub> $w ^ { \ast } \in [ 1 2 , 2 8 ]$ ; (2) Grid Models, consisting of seven Ising grids ranging from 10 × 10 to 80 × 80, with induced widths equal to the grid dimension; (3) Segmentation Models, including th<sub>ree</sub> i<sub>mage segmen</sub>t<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> i<sub>ns</sub>t<sub>ances w</sub>ith 231<sub>–</sub>235 <sub>var</sub>i<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>es an</sub>d $w ^ { * } \in [ 2 3 , 2 5 ]$ ; (4) Random Uniform Models, <sub>compr</sub>i<sub>s</sub>i<sub>ng seven syn</sub>th<sub>e</sub>ti<sub>c</sub> i<sub>ns</sub>t<sub>ances w</sub>ith 1094 <sub>var</sub>i<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>es an</sub>d hi<sub>g</sub>h i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>uce</sub>d <sub>w</sub>idth<sub>s</sub> $w ^ { \ast } \in [ 9 5 , 1 0 0 ]$ ; and (5) WCSP and Structured Prediction Models, consisting of seven instances, including protein folding tasks, to <sub>assess genera</sub>li<sub>za</sub>ti<sub>on across</sub> di<sub>verse op</sub>ti<sub>m</sub>i<sub>za</sub>ti<sub>on se</sub>tti<sub>ngs.</sub>

## F Implementation Details

## Data Generation Details

Gibbs Sampling. Training data is generated via parallel Gibbs sampling. We first find a feasible starting assignment using topological ordering for Bayesian networks. Each sample uses a burn-in of 2B steps (default B = 100) to ensure mixing. Partial assignment sizes follow an exponential distribution biased toward smaller sizes (bias <sub>p</sub>arameter 2.0), which im<sub>p</sub>roves covera<sub>g</sub>e of harder instances with more unassi<sub>g</sub>ned variables.

Data Splits. The full dataset is split as: 50% training, 10% validation, 20% calibration, 20% test. ## AOBB Integration

W<sub>e ex</sub>t<sub>en</sub>d DAOOPT <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>neura</sub>l b<sub>oun</sub>d <sub>quer</sub>i<sub>es v</sub>i<sub>a</sub> T<sub>orc</sub>hS<sub>cr</sub>i<sub>p</sub>t <sub>expor</sub>t<sub>.</sub> S<sub>pecu</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> l<sub>oo</sub>k<sub>a</sub>h<sub>ea</sub>d b<sub>a</sub>t<sub>c</sub>hi<sub>ng w</sub>ith de<sub>p</sub>th 8 <sub>g</sub>enerates u<sub>p</sub> to 256 <sub>p</sub>artial assi<sub>g</sub>nments <sub>p</sub>er batch, amortizin<sub>g</sub> GPU overhead. A two-level cache (L1 di<sub>rec</sub>t<sub>-mappe</sub>d <sub>w</sub>ith $2 ^ { 1 6 }$ entries, L2 hash map) achieves >95% hit rates. All experiments use OR-chain search with i-bound 4 for baseline MBE, auxiliary heuristics disabled, 10-minute budget, and fixed seed 42.

Anytime AOBB MAP improvement over MBE for SMNN (monotone-by-construction NN) and UMLP (unconstrained NN) on all 30 PGMs (single merged view; high-treewidth bucket on top, moderate/low-treewidth bucket below). ## G Computational Costs

L2B is an amortized method, trading time to train against time to infer. Label generation dominates: computing i-bound-10 labels costs 0.5–300 s per query, and a 20,000-example dataset therefore takes a one-time ≈ 3 h (Ba<sub>y</sub>esian networks) to ≈ 60 h (random-uniform), against which predictor training (≤ 10 min) and calibration (< 0.01 ms per query) are negligible. This upfront cost is justified precisely when a model is queried repeatedly: a standalone MBE-i4 bound already costs 35–235 s per query on the random-uniform family (Table 4), so the one-time training cost breaks even after roughly 1,000–6,000 queries—well within the regime of a solver that <sub>eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>t<sub>es a</sub> b<sub>oun</sub>d <sub>a</sub>t <sub>every searc</sub>h <sub>no</sub>d<sub>e.</sub> Thi<sub>s</sub> i<sub>s a</sub>l<sub>so w</sub>h<sub>y</sub> th<sub>e me</sub>th<sub>o</sub>d t<sub>arge</sub>t<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e amor</sub>ti<sub>ze</sub>d <sub>se</sub>tti<sub>ng ra</sub>th<sub>er</sub> th<sub>an</sub> <sub>one-s</sub>h<sub>o</sub>t i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>erence:</sub> f<sub>or a s</sub>i<sub>ng</sub>l<sub>e query one s</sub>h<sub>ou</sub>ld <sub>spen</sub>d th<sub>e</sub> b<sub>u</sub>d<sub>ge</sub>t <sub>on</sub> i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>erence</sub> di<sub>rec</sub>tl<sub>y,</sub> b<sub>u</sub>t b<sub>ranc</sub>h<sub>-an</sub>d<sub>-</sub>b<sub>oun</sub>d i<sub>ssues</sub> <sub>m</sub>illi<sub>ons</sub> <sub>o</sub>f b<sub>oun</sub>d <sub>eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>ti<sub>ons</sub> <sub>aga</sub>i<sub>ns</sub>t <sub>one</sub> fi<sub>xe</sub>d <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l <sub>so</sub> <sub>a</sub> l<sub>earne</sub>d b<sub>oun</sub>d i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>on</sub>l<sub>y</sub> <sub>rou</sub>t<sub>e</sub> t<sub>o</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>c</sub>h<sub>eap</sub> ti<sub>g</sub>ht b<sub>oun</sub>d <sub>a</sub>t <sub>a</sub>ll<sub>.</sub>

## H Timing Results

## I Alternative Encoding: Consecutive 2-Hot

The one-hot encoding used in the main paper allocates d+1 bits per variable of domain size d (d valueindicators plus one “unassigned” indicator). A natural alternative is the consecutive 2-hot encoding: for a variable of domain size d, allocate d+1 bits, and for observed value $k \in \{ 0 , \ldots , d { - } 1 \}$ set bits k and $k { + 1 }$ simultaneously; the unassigned state sets only bit d. Like one-hot, this encoding preserves the monotone <sub>re</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> b<sub>e</sub>t<sub>ween ev</sub>id<sub>ence grow</sub>th <sub>an</sub>d i<sub>npu</sub>t <sub>grow</sub>th <sub>requ</sub>i<sub>re</sub>d b<sub>y</sub> SMNN’<sub>s arc</sub>hit<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>ura</sub>l <sub>guaran</sub>t<sub>ee, so ca</sub>lib<sub>ra</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d 2-hot bounds remain valid by the same construction. Unlike one-hot, the 2-hot encoding induces an ordinal smoothness inductive bias: adjacent values share a bit, so the network is biased toward predictions that vary <sub>smoo</sub>thl<sub>y</sub> <sub>across</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>na</sub>t<sub>ura</sub>l <sub>or</sub>d<sub>er</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>o</sub>f th<sub>e</sub> d<sub>oma</sub>i<sub>n.</sub> W<sub>e</sub> i<sub>nc</sub>l<sub>u</sub>d<sub>e</sub> thi<sub>s</sub> <sub>exper</sub>i<sub>men</sub>t t<sub>o</sub> t<sub>es</sub>t <sub>w</sub>h<sub>e</sub>th<sub>er</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>uc</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> bi<sub>as</sub> t<sub>rans</sub>l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>es</sub> t<sub>o</sub> ti<sub>g</sub>ht<sub>er</sub> b<sub>oun</sub>d<sub>s,</sub> <sub>an</sub>d fi<sub>n</sub>d th<sub>a</sub>t it d<sub>oes—</sub>b<sub>u</sub>t <sub>on</sub>l<sub>y</sub> <sub>on</sub> PGM<sub>s</sub> <sub>w</sub>h<sub>ose</sub> <sub>un</sub>d<sub>er</sub>l<sub>y</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> f<sub>ac</sub>t<sub>ors</sub> <sub>carry</sub> <sub>or</sub>di<sub>na</sub>l <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>t<sub>ure.</sub>

Covera<sub>g</sub>e is <sub>p</sub>reserved across both encodin<sub>g</sub>s (Section above), so the <sub>g</sub>a<sub>p</sub> dif<sub>erences</sub> <sub>are</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>pure</sub> <sub>arc</sub>hit<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>ure</sub>/<sub>enco</sub>di<sub>ng</sub> <sub>e</sub>f<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>,</sub> <sub>no</sub>t <sub>a</sub> <sub>ca</sub>lib<sub>ra</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>ar</sub>tif<sub>ac</sub>t<sub>.</sub>

For PGMs with binary domains the encoding distinction collapses (both are 3 bits <sub>p</sub>er variable), and for PGMs with no a <sub>p</sub>riori ordinal structure on values, one-hot remains the safer default. ## J Monotone-Stratified Conformal Calibration

Empirically, this occurs on 63/3000 parent-child pairs in our verification, <sub>concen</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d i<sub>n</sub> PGM<sub>s</sub> <sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> $\hat { q } _ { \gamma } ^ { b }$ ramps steeply with b.

W<sub>e reso</sub>l<sub>ve</sub> thi<sub>s w</sub>ith <sub>a</sub> l<sub>e</sub>ft<sub>-</sub>t<sub>o-r</sub>i<sub>g</sub>ht <sub>mono</sub>t<sub>one cap on</sub> th<sub>e quan</sub>til<sub>e sc</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d<sub>u</sub>l<sub>e.</sub> Aft<sub>er compu</sub>ti<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e na</sub>t<sub>ura</sub>l <sub>per-</sub>bi<sub>n quan</sub>til<sub>es</sub> $\hat { q } _ { \gamma , \mathrm { s t r a t } } ^ { b }$ <sub>, we en</sub>f<sub>orce</sub>

$$
\hat { q } _ { \gamma , \mathrm { m o n o } } ^ { b } = \operatorname* { m i n } \left( \hat { q } _ { \gamma , \mathrm { m o n o } } ^ { b - 1 } , \ : \hat { q } _ { \gamma , \mathrm { s t r a t } } ^ { b } \right) , \quad \quad \hat { q } _ { \gamma , \mathrm { m o n o } } ^ { 0 } = \hat { q } _ { \gamma , \mathrm { s t r a t } } ^ { 0 } .\tag{3}
$$

The resulting schedule is non-increasing in b. Combined with SMNN’s architectural guarantee $f _ { \theta } ( x _ { E } ^ { \mathrm { c h i l d } } ) \leq$ $f _ { \theta } ( x _ { E } ^ { \mathrm { p a r e n t } } )$ ) and the search-induced ordering $b _ { \mathrm { c h i l d } } \geq b _ { \mathrm { p a r e n t } }$ <sub>,</sub> th<sub>e ca</sub>lib<sub>ra</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d b<sub>oun</sub>d <sub>sa</sub>ti<sub>s</sub>fi<sub>es</sub> $g ( x _ { E } ^ { \mathrm { c h i l d } } ) \leq g ( x _ { E } ^ { \mathrm { p a r e n t } } )$ deterministically along every search path. We verify this on 3000 randomly sampled parent-child pairs spanning 30 PGMs: SMNN with monotone-stratified calibration produces $0 / 3 0 0 0$ <sub>v</sub>i<sub>o</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ons, compare</sub>d t<sub>o</sub> 63/3000 under standard stratified conformal and $8 2 3 / 3 0 0 0$ <sub>un</sub>d<sub>er an uncons</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>ne</sub>d MLP <sub>arc</sub>hit<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>ure.</sub>

In branch-and-bound, deterministic monotonicity is the more important guarantee.

9: end if   
10<sub>:</sub> if x is complete assignment then

15<sub>:</sub> else

17<sub>:</sub> for each value $x _ { i }$ i<sub>n</sub> d<sub>oma</sub>i<sub>n o</sub>f $X _ { i }$ do

Table 14: Monotonicit<sub>y</sub>-alon<sub>g</sub>-AOBB-<sub>p</sub>aths verification. For each PGM we sam<sub>p</sub>le 100 random <sub>p</sub>arent→child evidence <sub>p</sub>airs (child = <sub>p</sub>arent + one additional variable) and count <sub>p</sub>airs where bound(child) > bound(<sub>p</sub>arent), which would violate the admissibility of upper-bounding heuristics in AOBB. Raw = direct neural prediction (the architectural property). Calibrated = stratified-conformal at $\gamma = 0 . 9 9 9$ (the de<sub>p</sub>lo<sub>y</sub>ed bound). SMNN raw violations are zero b<sub>y</sub> construction (monotone-b<sub>y</sub>-desi<sub>g</sub>n with one-hot encodin<sub>g</sub>); the <sub>p</sub>revious stratified conformal wrapper introduced 63/3000 calibrated violations from per-bin q jumps at evidence-size bin b<sub>oun</sub>d<sub>ar</sub>i<sub>es.</sub> Th<sub>e new mono</sub>t<sub>one-cap ca</sub>lib<sub>ra</sub>t<sub>or</sub> $q _ { \mathrm { m o n o } } [ b ] = \mathrm { m i n } ( q _ { \mathrm { m o n o } } [ b - 1 ] , q _ { \mathrm { s t r a t } } [ b ] )$ <sub>en</sub>f<sub>orces a non-</sub> increasing q schedule across bins, eliminating these wrapper-induced violations and yielding 0/3000 calibrated SMNN violations across all 30 PGMs. UMLP is unconstrained and frequently violates the <sub>proper</sub>t<sub>y a</sub>t b<sub>o</sub>th <sub>raw an</sub>d <sub>ca</sub>lib<sub>ra</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d l<sub>eve</sub>l<sub>s.</sub>
